Enhancing decision-making with linear diophantine multi-fuzzy set: application of novel information measures in medical and engineering fields
Authors: Jeevitha Kannan, Vimala Jayakumar, Nasreen Kausar, Dragan Pamucar, Vladimir Simic
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
This study offers a comprehensive analysis of novel information for linear diophantine multi-fuzzy sets and illustrates its applications in practical scenarios. We introduce innovative similarity metrics tailored for linear diophantine multi-fuzzy sets, including Cosine similarity, Jaccard similarity, and Exponential similarity. Additionally, we propose Entropy, Inclusion, and Distance measures, providing a robust theoretical foundation supported by developed theorems that explain the interactions between these metrics. The practical implications of these theoretical advancements are demonstrated through various case studies. Specifically, we apply the similarity measures to predict preeclampsia, a severe condition affecting pregnant women, showcasing their potential in medical diagnostics. The entropy measure is used to identify the optimal materials manufacturing method for medical surgical robots, underscoring its importance in ensuring patient safety and the effectiveness of medical procedures. Furthermore, the inclusion measure is employed in pattern recognition tasks, highlighting its utility in complex data analysis. The comparative and superiority analysis shows the effectiveness of our research. The novel aspect of this study is the implementation of information metrics for LDMFS. These efforts aim to enhance the impact and practical applicability of linear diophantine multi-fuzzy sets, fostering innovation and improving outcomes across multiple fields.

Introduction

The existence of several types of uncertainties in the data, which may stem from ignorance or human error, makes it difficult for people to choose the optimal course of action in a variety of situations. Numerous theories are used to assess these risks and the process itself, including the fuzzy set theory1 and its expansions, including the intuitionistic fuzzy set (IFS)2, Pythagorean fuzzy set (PFS)3, the q-rung orthopair fuzzy set (q-ROFS)4. Hamacher Aggregation Operators for PFS were introduced by Asif et al.5, and they were used in MCDM issues. An MCDM technique for intuitionistic fuzzy soft information was proposed by Ali et al.6. An MCGDM method for Robot Selection Using an Interval-Valued Intuitionistic Fuzzy Set was introduced by Imran et al.7. Sustainable wastewater treatment technologies were examined by Mishra et al.8, utilising an interval-valued intuitionistic fuzzy technique. According to each of these theories, experts assess an object using its two membership degrees to make sure that its sum, square sum, and qth power of sum are all equal to one.

Because reference parameters exist, the linear diophantine fuzzy set (LDFS)9, the forerunner of a new and distinct fuzzy idea, eliminates all of these limitations. Jeevitha10 advocated for and used the DEMATEL approach in the context of climate change given the LDF environment. Jeevitha et al11 is credited with developing the linear diophantine multi-fuzzy aggregation operators and applying them to digital transformation. Vimala constructed the sophisticated LDF soft set12 in order to select the appropriate Agri-Drone. In Aiyared Iampam13, several Einstein aggregation approaches were used to discuss LDFS for MCDM problems. These operators can determine the best option and derive ranking information. Afterward, Saya Ayub14 used decision-making to connect algebraic features with LDF relations. By proposing the concept of soft rough sets for use in material handling equipment, Riaz15 expanded on LDFS. Riaz16 created aggregation operators (AOs) that employed linear Diophantine fuzzy numbers (LDFNs) in priority order to select third-party logistic service providers. Farid17 suggested Einstein’s prioritized linear Diophantine fuzzy AOs with applications.

Frank AOs for linear Diophantine fuzzy numbers with interval values were recently constructed by Riaz18. In addition to outlining the features and applications of LDFS, Vimala et al.19 developed the MARCOS method for LDFS. The LDFS clustering approach was explained by Jeevitha et al.20, who used the LDF correlation coefficient. Petchimuthu21 sought to leverage IVLDF data and its AOs to solve the supplier selection problem. The multi-fuzzy soft set was implemented in LDFS by Jeevitha22, who also discussed its applicability in the tender selection process. In addition, the LDMFS similarity measure was presented and applied to choose the best substitute for the petrol23. Also, the lattice structures are implemented in LDFS24,25. The LDF-CODAS approach was established by Kannan et al.26 in order to choose the best logistics provider.

In contrast, Sebastian27 coined the phrase “multi-fuzzy set” to refer to the fuzzy sets multiple membership technique. Every element can have a distinct membership value in a variety of scenarios. To address these types of uncertainty, multi-fuzzy sets are presented. Eventually, dissatisfaction grades were added to the intuitionistic multi-fuzzy set28. Additionally, the multi-fuzzy set extension was used to discuss the forecasting process29. The analysis on q-rung multi-fuzzy set was introduced by Mahalakshmi et al.30.

Literature review

This subsection covers existing research on inclusion, similarity, and entropy measures.

Inclusion measure

The degree to which one fuzzy set is encompassed within another is indicated by the fuzzy set inclusion measure. Subsequently, Cornelis31 added an intuitionistic fuzzy set and amended Sinha’s postulate. Bustince32 examined the fuzzy set’s interval-valued inclusion criterion. The concepts of L-fuzzy valued inclusion measure, L-fuzzy valued similarity measure, and L-fuzzy distance was introduced by Kehagias33.

Numerous academics have thoroughly examined inclusion measures, which are suggested based on gradual interactions between things. A measure of inclusion34 expresses how much one element of a poset is contained in another. The inclusion measure displays the precise pair-wise inclusion degree between any two elements in addition to reflecting inclusion relations between two elements that have a partial relation in a poset. The majority of metrics in rough set theory are inclusion measures, as demonstrated by Xu et al.35. Thus, using inclusion-measure-based techniques is a crucial procedure. Subsequent research has examined the inclusion measure in intuitionistic environments. A spherical fuzzy aggregation operator was used in real-world applications by Hussain et al.36. Spherical fuzzy was employed by Biswas et al.37 to compare SMES preparation in quality 4.0. The MEREC approach for fuzzy sets employing parabolic measurements was introduced by Narang et al.38.

The constructive method works well for real-world applications of inclusionary practices. Young39 and Fan et al.40 suggested using inclusion measures to improve clustering validity. The semantic inclusion degree in Ma et al.41 proposed the assessment criterion of data redundancy in fuzzy relational databases. An uncertainty analysis approach with various inclusion measures for intelligent systems and other systems, including fuzzy relational databases, was discussed by Qiu et al.42. Rough set theory has also incorporated the idea of inclusion measures, and Xu et al.43 have established several significant connections between inclusion measures and measures of rough set data processing. Fan et al.44 have also effectively integrated the inclusion measure into fuzzy concept lattice theory. However, the axiomatic method is suitable for examining the inclusion measure’s structure. This method characterises the inclusion operators using a set of axioms. In 1987, Kitainik45 originally put out four axioms for the inclusion measure based on the features of crisp inclusion relations. Zhang and Leung46 proposed a generalised definition for the inclusion measure, called including degree, to represent and measure the uncertainty information because they believed that quantitative methods were the main approaches in uncertainty inference, which is a crucial problem for artificial intelligence. A more generalised inclusion measure was proposed by Fan et al.40 by altering the monotonicity of Young’s axioms.

Similarity measure

An essential tool for determining how similar two objects are to one another is a similarity measure. Since measures of similarity across fuzzy sets are essential to fuzzy mathematics and have numerous applications in a wide range of domains, researchers are beginning to focus on them. In recent years, a variety of similarity measures that are extremely helpful in specific domains, like pattern recognition, machine learning, decision-making, and image processing, have been proposed and studied for similarity across fuzzy sets. First to put up the idea of a similarity metric for FS was Wang et al47. The applicability of the FS similarity measure to IFS48, PFS49, and other systems was investigated following multiple studies on the measure50,51. Majumdar and Samanta52 addressed certain similarity measures for both soft sets and FSS. Later on, PFSS53 and IFSS54 were added to it.

Entropy measure

Entropy is a key concept in fuzzy set theory. Fuzzy sets’ degree and fuzziness are both described by their entropy. Luca and Termini55 introduced the axiom formulation of fuzzy set entropy. They also acknowledged Shannon’s probability entropy and viewed it as an information-gathering measure. Kaufmann56 pointed out that the entropy of a fuzzy set may be found by measuring the distance between the fuzzy set and its nearest non-fuzzy set, whereas Higashi and Klir57 used the distance from a fuzzy set to its complement. Trillas and Riera58 offered general expressions for this entropy, and Loo59 offered a definition of entropy that combines definitions from Kaufmann56 and Luca and Termini55. Liu60 gave the widely accepted definitions of fuzzy similarity, distance, and entropy. Fan and Ma61 offered a comprehensive conclusion on fuzzy entropy induced by distance measure based on the axiom definitions of fuzzy entropy and distance measure. The entropy of intuitionistic and interval-valued fuzzy sets has been examined by some authors. Zhang et al.62 proposed a unique definition of entropy for interval-valued fuzzy sets based on the distance by utilizing the axiomatic definition of entropy of a fuzzy set given by Luca and Termini55 and Liu60. The AROMAN MCDM Approach was used in the Healthcare System Devolution Strategy by Bouraima et al.63.

The entropy of intuitionistic and interval-valued fuzzy sets has been examined by several scholars. Burillo and Bustince64 introduced the concept of entropy of intuitionistic fuzzy sets, which allows one to calculate the degree of fuzziness of an intuitionistic fuzzy set. Szmidt and Kacprzyk65 proposed an entropy measure of a nonprobilistic kind with a geometric interpretation of intuitionistic fuzzy sets. Zeng and Li66 developed the notion of interval-valued fuzzy sets to clarify the assumptions of Szmidt and Kacprzyk65 and investigated the relationship between similarity measures and entropy. Using the concept of probability, Hung and Yang62 were able to define the fuzzy entropy of intuitionistic fuzzy sets. They also created the axiom’s definition and its characteristics.

Research motivation

This study is driven by the need to improve fuzzy set theory in order to handle scenarios involving decision-making that are becoming more intricate and complex. This work attempts to give more precise and flexible analytical tools by adding new information and similarity metrics for linear diophantine multi-fuzzy sets. The particular use in preeclampsia prediction, surgical robot material optimization, and enhanced pattern recognition highlights the applicability and potential influence of these theoretical developments in real-world settings.

Key highlights of this study

Objective of this study

This work aims to present and evaluate novel criteria for similarity and information for linear diophantine multi-fuzzy sets (LDMFS). This study aims to show, through two crucial applications, the practical significance and influence of these metrics. First, by applying the recently created similarity metrics, the study seeks to enhance the prediction of preeclampsia, a dangerous illness that affects pregnant women. This application highlights how these methods might improve the precision and dependability of medical diagnoses, which will ultimately lead to improved outcomes for the health of mothers and fetuses. The study delves into the application of the entropy measure in determining the most effective materials manufacturing techniques for medical surgical robots. This application emphasizes how crucial the suggested actions are in guaranteeing patient safety and the effectiveness of medical treatments by determining which materials are most suited for production.

Through detailed analysis and discussion of these applications, the research aims to show how widely applicable and useful the suggested LDMFS measurements are in a variety of sectors. This all-encompassing strategy seeks to stimulate creativity and enhance results, ultimately augmenting the total usefulness and influence of linear diophantine multi-fuzzy sets in practice.

Research gap

The literature review reveals several significant gaps that this research aims to address. Specifically, research on information measures for linear diophantine multi-fuzzy sets (LDMFS) is conspicuously lacking. We suggest novel information and similarity measures designed specifically for LDMFS to bridge this gap. Moreover, despite the paucity of study on LDMFS, this mathematical framework has a wide range of possible applications. This paper investigates the usefulness of LDMFS in important real-world circumstances.

Additionally, no prior research has been done on predicting preeclampsia using LDMFS. This work uses the suggested LDMFS measurements to create a predictive model for preeclampsia, a dangerous condition for which early and accurate prediction is crucial to the health of both the mother and the fetus.

Structure of this study

The structure of this study is organized to provide a comprehensive understanding of the proposed information measures and their applications. Section “Literature review”, titled “Preliminaries,” offers fundamental definitions that lay the groundwork for the study. Section “Information measures for linear diophantine multi-fuzzy set” is divided into three subsections: similarity measure, entropy measure, and inclusion measure, each detailing the respective methodologies and their theoretical underpinnings. Section “Practical applications: enhancing decision-making in medical and engineering fields” delves into practical applications and presents a case study to demonstrate the utility of the proposed information measures in real-world scenarios. Finally, section “Superiority analysis” consists of a superiority analysis of our research from three different perspectives. Also, it discusses limitations and managerial implications. The last section concludes the study, summarizing the key findings and implications of the research.

_{\mathfrak {Y}}^{i})^{\lambda }, 1-(1-\beta _{\mathfrak {Y}}^{i})^{\lambda }\rangle \end{aligned} \right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Information measures for linear diophantine multi-fuzzy set

These four innovative information measures-distance, similarity, entropy, and inclusion-for LDMFS are covered in this section. The discussion also includes the axioms and theorems.

Definition 8

A real-valued function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {S}$$\end{document} : LDMFS(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document}) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times$$\end{document} LDMFS(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document}) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} [0,1] is called Similarity measures between two LDMFS \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}, if it fulfills the successive specifications:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le S(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le S(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_3)$$\end{document}.for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le S(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le S(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = S(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_1)$$\end{document}.

Theorem 1

Consider\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_1 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_2 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(j = 1,2,3,...,n) be two LDMFSs. Then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2)$$\end{document} is as follows:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = 1-\frac{1}{4m} \sum _{i=1}^{m} \Bigg (\begin{aligned} max_{j}|\mu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j} (\mathfrak {l}_i) - \mu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)| + max_{j}|\nu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) - \nu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)| \\+ max_{j}|\alpha _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) - \alpha _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)| + max_{j}|\beta _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) - \beta _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)|\end{aligned}\Bigg ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

See Appendix.A\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Definition 9

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document} = {\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {l_1,l_2,l_3,...,l_r}$$\end{document}} be the universal set and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_1 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_2 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(j = 1,2,3,...,n) be the two LDMFS over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document}. Then the Cosine Similarity Measure(CSM) of two LDMFS is symbolized as follows:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = \frac{1}{m} \sum _{i=1}^{m} \left\{ \frac{1}{n}\sum _{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\mu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) + \nu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\nu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) + \alpha _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\alpha _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) + \beta _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\beta _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)}{\left\{ \begin{aligned}\sqrt{(\mu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2 + (\nu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2 + (\alpha _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2 + (\beta _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2}\\ \sqrt{(\mu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2 + (\nu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2 + (\alpha _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2 + (\beta _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2}\end{aligned}\right\} }\right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Theorem 2

Consider\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_1 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_2 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}be two LDMFSs. Then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2)$$\end{document} holds the successive specifications:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_1)$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = 1$$\end{document}, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 = \mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}.If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_2 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_3$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_3)$$\end{document}.for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_1)$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_1)$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = 1$$\end{document}, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 = \mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = 1$$\end{document}, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 = \mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}.

Definition 10

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document} = {\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {l_1,l_2,l_3,...,l_r}$$\end{document}} be the universal set and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_1 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_2 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(j = 1,2,3,...,n) be the two LDMFS over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document}. Then the Jaccard Similarity Measure(JSM) of two LDMFS is symbolized as follows:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = \frac{1}{m} \sum _{i=1}^{m} \left\{ \frac{1}{n}\sum _{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\mu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) + \nu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\nu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) + \alpha _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\alpha _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) + \beta _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\beta _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)}{\left\{ \begin{aligned}+ \\ [(\mu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2 + (\nu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2 + (\alpha _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2 + (\beta _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i))^2]-\\ [\mu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\mu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) + \nu _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\nu _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) + \alpha _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\alpha _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) + \beta _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)\beta _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)]\end{aligned}\right\} }\right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Theorem 3

Consider\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_1 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_2 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(j = 1,2,3,...,n) be two LDMFSs. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} {L}_2) = \mathbb {CSM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_1)$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = 1$$\end{document}, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 = \mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}.If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_2 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_3$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le \mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_3)$$\end{document}.for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = 1$$\end{document}, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 = \mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}.

Definition 11

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} max_{j}|\beta _{\mathscr {L}_1}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i) - \beta _{\mathscr {L}_2}^{j}(\mathfrak {l}_i)|\end{aligned}\Bigg )\Bigg ]} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Theorem 4

Consider\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_1 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\mathscr {L}_2 = \{(\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle ): \mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(j = 1,2,3,...,n) be two LDMFSs. Then the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2)$$\end{document} holds the successive specifications:

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_1)$$\end{document}.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = 1$$\end{document}, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 = \mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}.If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_2 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_3$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_3)$$\end{document}.for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) \le 1$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_1)$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_1)$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = 1$$\end{document}, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 = \mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {JSM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2) = 1$$\end{document}, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 = \mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document}.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_2 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_3$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_3)$$\end{document}.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_2 \subseteq \mathscr {L}_3$$\end{document}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_2)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_1, \mathscr {L}_3) \le \mathbb {ESM}(\mathscr {L}_2, \mathscr {L}_3)$$\end{document}.

Example 1

Theorem 5

Consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_1$$\end{document} = {(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_1}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle$$\end{document}): \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta$$\end{document}} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {L}_2$$\end{document} = {(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {l}_i, \langle (\mu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \nu ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle , \langle (\alpha ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i), \beta ^{j}_{\mathscr {L}_2}(\mathfrak {l}_i) \rangle )$$\end{document}: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {l}_i \in \Delta$$\end{document}} (j = 1,2,3,...,n) be two LDMFSs. Practical applications: enhancing decision-making in medical and engineering fields

In this section, the applications of our proposed information measures for LDMFS are discussed.

Application on similarity measures

Case study on preclampsia

Disorders associated with placental dysfunction (PDDs), such as intrauterine growth restriction and preeclampsia, need prompt referral choices. The primary cause of maternal death and morbidity worldwide is preeclampsia67. This dangerous medical condition, which can arise after 20 weeks of pregnancy, can put the mother, fetus, and infant at great risk68. Preeclampsia, which affects 3–7% of pregnancies overall, is typified by the advent of new proteinuria and gestational hypertension, usually during the third trimester69. It puts the health of the mother in peril, but it also stunts the growth of the fetus, which results in low birth weights, which are a major cause of neonatal death [?]. Preeclampsia is characterized by swelling, headaches, impaired vision, elevated blood pressure, protein in the urine, and stress on the mother’s heart and other organs. Along with other serious side effects, this illness damages the placenta’s blood supply from the mother, damages the kidneys and liver, and accumulates fluid in the lungs70. Preeclampsia affects roughly 5.37 out of every 10,000 pregnancies in Saudi Arabia. It may have negative effects on the pregnancy, such as neurological problems for the unborn child68. Pregnant women who have preeclampsia have no recognised conclusive treatment; the exact pathophysiology and aetiology of the condition are unknown. Therefore, in order to avoid difficulties and enhance outcomes, early diagnosis of preeclampsia risk prior to pregnancy is essential71.

Preeclampsia is defined as blood pressure of at least 140/90 mm Hg in two distinct stages separated by at least four hours, as well as proteinuria of at least 0.3 g in the urine collected within 24 hours of each other. Preeclampsia often starts after the 20th week of pregnancy72. One of the main causes of maternal and fetal mortality globally, this condition affects 5-8% of pregnancies73–75. Preeclampsia prevalence varies throughout Iran; reports indicate that it is 10% in urban regions and 4% in rural ones76. Renal necrosis, pulmonary edema, liver rupture, hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, reduced platelet syndrome, and stroke are among the problems that can arise from preeclampsia77.

A study from 2021 estimates that the prevalence of preeclampsia varies by state and area in India, with an approximate value of 28%.Preeclampsia prevalence was shown to be similar in India’s rural and urban areas in a 2014 study; however, greater rates were noted in certain states, with Tripura having the highest rate at 87.5%. According to a 2023 study, the risk of preeclampsia in pregnant women with a family history of hypertension is 4.152 times higher. Symptoms: The symptoms of preeclampsia can range from mild to severe and may include1. The symptoms are derived from the literature study on the Preeclampsia Foundation’s official website78 (Refer Figure. 1).

Fig. 1Symptoms of preeclampsia.

Symptoms of preeclampsia.

Hypertension: Blood pressure readings of 140/90 mm Hg or higher on two occasions at least four hours apart.Proteinuria: Excess protein in the urine (greater than 300 mg in a 24-hour urine sample).Edema: Swelling, particularly in the hands, feet, and face.Severe headaches: Persistent headaches that do not respond to usual analgesics.Visual disturbances: Blurred vision, seeing spots, or light sensitivity.Upper abdominal pain: Usually under the ribs on the right side.Nausea or vomiting: Not usually associated with typical morning sickness.Shortness of breath: Caused by lung fluid.Treatment: There is no cure for preeclampsia other than delivery of the baby. However, management strategies can help mitigate the risks and complications:

Hypertension: Blood pressure readings of 140/90 mm Hg or higher on two occasions at least four hours apart.

Hypertension: Blood pressure readings of 140/90 mm Hg or higher on two occasions at least four hours apart.

Proteinuria: Excess protein in the urine (greater than 300 mg in a 24-hour urine sample).

Proteinuria: Excess protein in the urine (greater than 300 mg in a 24-hour urine sample).

Edema: Swelling, particularly in the hands, feet, and face.

Edema: Swelling, particularly in the hands, feet, and face.

Severe headaches: Persistent headaches that do not respond to usual analgesics.

Severe headaches: Persistent headaches that do not respond to usual analgesics.

Visual disturbances: Blurred vision, seeing spots, or light sensitivity.

Visual disturbances: Blurred vision, seeing spots, or light sensitivity.

Upper abdominal pain: Usually under the ribs on the right side.

Upper abdominal pain: Usually under the ribs on the right side.

Nausea or vomiting: Not usually associated with typical morning sickness.

Nausea or vomiting: Not usually associated with typical morning sickness.

Shortness of breath: Caused by lung fluid.

Algorithm

Step 1: Define the alternative and parameter set.Step 2: Construct an LDMFS model for a Preeclampsia patient.Step 3: Construct an LDMFS for all patients to be checked.Step 4: Compute the similarity measures (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}$$\end{document}4, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {JSM}$$\end{document}5, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}$$\end{document}6) between the model and other patients.Step 5: Rank the similarity measures.

Step 1: Define the alternative and parameter set.

Step 2: Construct an LDMFS model for a Preeclampsia patient.

Step 2: Construct an LDMFS model for a Preeclampsia patient.

Step 3: Construct an LDMFS for all patients to be checked.

Methodological framework and calculation procedures

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}$$\end{document} = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{W_1, W_2, W_3, W_4\}$$\end{document} be the set of Pregnant women and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}$$\end{document} = {Hypertension(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_1$$\end{document}), Proteinuria(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_2$$\end{document}), Edema(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_3$$\end{document}), Severe Headaches(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_4$$\end{document}), Visual Disturbances(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_5$$\end{document}), Upper Abdominal Pain(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_6$$\end{document}), Nausea(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_7$$\end{document}), Shortness of Breath(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_8$$\end{document}), sudden weight gain(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_9$$\end{document})} be the set of symptoms of Preeclampsia, taken as a alternative set. The symptoms are monitored at a certain period. 11th week, 12th week, 13th week are taken as indices. Also, the model for Preeclampsia is constructed as W. The LDMFS values are shown in the Tables 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5.

To find the patient of preeclampsia, we utilize the similarity measure for LDMFS(??. The similarity measure between the model W and the pregnant women(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_1, W_2, W_3, W_4$$\end{document}) is calculated as below:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathscr {S}(W,W_1) =&1- \frac{1}{4.9} \left\{ \begin{aligned} + [0.36+0.27+0.26+0.17] +\\ [0.43+0.51+0.09+0.03]+[0.46+0.38+0.18+0.20]+\\ [0.18+0.25+0.09+0.13] + [0.44+0.46+0.12+0.13]+\\ [0.39 + 0.42+0.20+0.13]+[0.34+0.44+0.33+0.19] \end{aligned}\right\} \\ =&1- \frac{1}{36}(9.29)\\ =&1- 0.2581\\ =&0.7419 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Similarly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {S}(W,W_2)$$\end{document} = 0.7036, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {S}(W,W_3)$$\end{document} = 0.8439, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {S}(W,W_4)$$\end{document} = 0.70305

Also, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {CSM}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {JSM}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {ESM}$$\end{document} are calculated and shown in the Table 6. Result and discussion

Based on the calculated results, it is evident that patient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_3$$\end{document} is affected by preeclampsia. Therefore, patient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_3$$\end{document} requires medication specific to the treatment of preeclampsia. It is important to note that many women are not well-informed about this condition and often assume that feeling unwell is a normal part of pregnancy. However, it is crucial to be vigilant about these symptoms.

Comparative analysis of proposed SM with previaling SM.

Based on these studies and the comparative analysis, we draw the conclusion from the current research that the results derived using the suggested technique are compatible with the current research, making it conservative. The main advantage of the proposed technique over the current methods for decision-making, however, is in the fact that it has a lot more data to interpret the ambiguities in the data. Since it conveys object information more accurately and objectively under LDMFS, it is a useful tool for dealing with ambiguous and imprecise information throughout the decision-making process.

Application on entropy measures

Case study on mini surgical robots

With a focus on manufacturing applications, the “Manufacturing Robotics Report”83 offers a thorough overview of the field’s present and future developments in robotics. It examines key trends and technologies, with a particular emphasis on material innovations like soft robotics, which open up new possibilities for jobs requiring dexterity Fig. 3. The integration of Industry 4.0 technologies is covered in the report, with particular emphasis on how AI, IoT, and cyber-physical systems are revolutionising industrial processes, removing obstacles for smaller businesses, and increasing productivity. A bibliometric analysis of material selection using MCDM methods was carried out by Sahoo et al84. It also discusses the emerging significance of in-hardware learning and high parallelism of neuromorphic computing for robots. The report’s overall goal is to help engineers better grasp the potential and complexities present in the rapidly developing field of robotics production.

Fig. 3Properties of robots.

Properties of robots.

The materials used in small surgical robots have a big impact on the patient’s health as well as the robot’s performance in a few different ways:

Robotic performanceBiocompatibility: Materials should be biocompatible with tissues to avoid negative reactions. Materials that are non-reactive lower the possibility of rejection, infection, or inflammation.Strength and durability: Materials for surgical robots must be strong enough to endure the mechanical strains involved in surgery without deteriorating. This guarantees accuracy and dependability when performing procedures.Flexibility and miniaturisation: The materials must have the requisite flexibility and be able to be miniaturised. These requirements can be met by designing polymers and specific alloys, which allow for complex movements in small places.Sterilizability: The materials must be able to withstand sterilisation procedures, including autoclaving, without deteriorating. Maintaining hygienic standards in surgery depends on this.Precision and control: The robot’s precision is influenced by the material’s characteristics. Fine materials help create the precise and controlled movements required for intricate surgical operations.Patient Health:Reduced trauma: Compared to traditional surgery, minimally invasive operations are frequently made possible by small surgical robots, which results in less trauma. The size and design of the robot are influenced by the materials used, which also has an effect on how invasive the treatment is.Infection risk: Materials that are sterilizable and biocompatible reduce the chance of infection. Materials that are cleanable and non-reactive stop the growth of germs and other diseases.Healing and recovery: Materials that encourage biocompatibility and lessen inflammation can hasten the healing and recovery processes. Certain coatings, for instance, can improve tissue integration or lessen scarring.Allergic responses and toxicity: To avoid negative responses, use hypoallergenic and non-toxic products. Certain people may experience allergies to metals like nickel, however titanium is renowned for having exceptional biocompatibility.Postoperative complications: Reductions in implant rejection, material degradation, or unfavourable tissue reactions are possible with superior materials.The materials that go into making tiny surgical robots are essential to their successful operation, patient safety, and overall outcome. The capabilities and security of these robotic systems are constantly being enhanced by developments in material science. Understanding the necessity of a part manufacturing process with tight tolerance and high precision and accuracy is a prerequisite for manufacturing medical robots. This is due to the fact that medical robots handle human life, and even a minor error could result in fatalities or further difficulties.

Biocompatibility: Materials should be biocompatible with tissues to avoid negative reactions. Materials that are non-reactive lower the possibility of rejection, infection, or inflammation.

Biocompatibility: Materials should be biocompatible with tissues to avoid negative reactions. Materials that are non-reactive lower the possibility of rejection, infection, or inflammation.

Strength and durability: Materials for surgical robots must be strong enough to endure the mechanical strains involved in surgery without deteriorating. This guarantees accuracy and dependability when performing procedures.

Flexibility and miniaturisation: The materials must have the requisite flexibility and be able to be miniaturised. These requirements can be met by designing polymers and specific alloys, which allow for complex movements in small places.

Sterilizability: The materials must be able to withstand sterilisation procedures, including autoclaving, without deteriorating. Maintaining hygienic standards in surgery depends on this.

Sterilizability: The materials must be able to withstand sterilisation procedures, including autoclaving, without deteriorating. Maintaining hygienic standards in surgery depends on this.

Precision and control: The robot’s precision is influenced by the material’s characteristics. Fine materials help create the precise and controlled movements required for intricate surgical operations.

Precision and control: The robot’s precision is influenced by the material’s characteristics. Fine materials help create the precise and controlled movements required for intricate surgical operations.

Reduced trauma: Compared to traditional surgery, minimally invasive operations are frequently made possible by small surgical robots, which results in less trauma. The size and design of the robot are influenced by the materials used, which also has an effect on how invasive the treatment is.

Reduced trauma: Compared to traditional surgery, minimally invasive operations are frequently made possible by small surgical robots, which results in less trauma. The size and design of the robot are influenced by the materials used, which also has an effect on how invasive the treatment is.

Infection risk: Materials that are sterilizable and biocompatible reduce the chance of infection. Materials that are cleanable and non-reactive stop the growth of germs and other diseases.

Infection risk: Materials that are sterilizable and biocompatible reduce the chance of infection. Materials that are cleanable and non-reactive stop the growth of germs and other diseases.

Healing and recovery: Materials that encourage biocompatibility and lessen inflammation can hasten the healing and recovery processes. Certain coatings, for instance, can improve tissue integration or lessen scarring.

Healing and recovery: Materials that encourage biocompatibility and lessen inflammation can hasten the healing and recovery processes. Certain coatings, for instance, can improve tissue integration or lessen scarring.

Allergic responses and toxicity: To avoid negative responses, use hypoallergenic and non-toxic products. Certain people may experience allergies to metals like nickel, however titanium is renowned for having exceptional biocompatibility.

Allergic responses and toxicity: To avoid negative responses, use hypoallergenic and non-toxic products. Certain people may experience allergies to metals like nickel, however titanium is renowned for having exceptional biocompatibility.

Postoperative complications: Reductions in implant rejection, material degradation, or unfavourable tissue reactions are possible with superior materials.

Postoperative complications: Reductions in implant rejection, material degradation, or unfavourable tissue reactions are possible with superior materials.

The following are typical manufacturing procedures used to create medical robots:Insert moulding and overmolding: These injection moulding techniques are appropriate for producing items made of two materials, either the same or different. In the two-step manufacturing process of overmolding, an item is created utilising two different or identical plastic materials. The first section is created by the operator inside an injection mould, and the finished product is then placed in another. The operator then uses two injection moulding operations to create a single product by moulding the second portion. Overmolding and insert moulding are comparable. But the plastic is moulded onto an insert, which is a non-plastic substance that is typically metal. It is an excellent technique for producing fasteners for medical robots.Precision CNC turning: When it comes to producing medical robots precisely and with little tolerance, CNC (Computer Numerical Control) machining is the ideal choice. CAD software is used in this computer-controlled subtractive manufacturing technique to make parts. The procedure can be used to create end effectors, screws, sensors, and other elements for medical robots. The following are some reasons why medical robot manufacturing finds precision CNC machining to be extremely suitable. CNC turning and milling are two examples of extremely quick CNC machining procedures. The method allows for the rapid manufacture of medical robot components without sacrificing accuracy, whether they are produced in small quantities or individually. When creating intricate shapes with precise tolerances, CNC machining is a highly useful technique. To fulfill their intended purposes, the majority of medical robot components have intricate designs.The 3D printing process: Using CAD models and 3D printers, additive manufacturing processes such as 3D printing create sturdy, three-dimensional objects. It is the most widely used manufacturing technique for producing medical parts, and it can be used to produce robot components such as robotic prostheses. A few advantages of 3D printing for the production of medical parts are listed below: Quick Prototyping and Adaptability.Before deciding on a machining process for medical robots, there are a few things to take into account. Choosing the appropriate medical machining process is crucial. These machining processes are taken from the literature survey on the Wayken Rapid Manufacturing website85 and 2023 manufacturing robotics reports86. Among these factors are: Protyping: Compared to Real-time Manufacturing, Prototyping During the early stages of manufacturing, prototype manufacturing entails creating small-scale pieces for testing. 3D printing and CNC machining are typically used to do this. Use low-volume CNC machining or injection molding for manufacturing in real time.Precision and intolerance: Medical robot components need to be extremely precise and accurate when put together. Thus, to reduce errors, manufacturing processes must have strict tolerances. All of the previously mentioned techniques are appropriate in terms of accuracy. Automation, however, prioritizes CNC machining over other methods.Suitability for complex designs: Complex designs are found in robotic parts. You therefore require a manufacturing procedure that can work with intricate patterns. The best techniques in this case are CNC machining and 3D printing. Because 5-axis CNC machines can rotate and move along many axes to construct the final design, they are generally the best choice for producing complex robotic parts.

Insert moulding and overmolding: These injection moulding techniques are appropriate for producing items made of two materials, either the same or different. In the two-step manufacturing process of overmolding, an item is created utilising two different or identical plastic materials. The first section is created by the operator inside an injection mould, and the finished product is then placed in another. The operator then uses two injection moulding operations to create a single product by moulding the second portion. Overmolding and insert moulding are comparable. But the plastic is moulded onto an insert, which is a non-plastic substance that is typically metal. It is an excellent technique for producing fasteners for medical robots.

Methodological framework and calculation procedures

Let us consider the three manufacturing process of mini-surgical robots as {\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1$$\end{document}(Insert moulding and overmolding),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_2$$\end{document}(Precision CNC Turning),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_3$$\end{document} (The 3D Printing Process)} as LDMFS. The factors of machining process are taken as a alternative set {\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1$$\end{document}(Protyping), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_2$$\end{document}(Precision and Intolerance), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_3$$\end{document}(Suitability for Complex Designs)}.
